Finger-actuated microfluidic device for point-of-care circulating cell-free nucleic acid extraction.

Musaddaq G, Ehsan L, Yoga Y et al.. Finger-actuated microfluidic device for point-of-care circulating cell-free nucleic acid extraction.. Lab on a chip. 2026

This paper developed a fully finger-actuated microfluidic device designed to extract cell-free nucleic acids from plasma without requiring any external power source or laboratory pipetting equipment. The device integrates manually operated valves, a pump, and onboard reagent storage to perform a complete extraction workflow—including lysis, binding, washing, and elution—in roughly half an hour. Performance was benchmarked against both manual benchtop and automated robotic extraction methods, with the device achieving DNA recoveries comparable to the commercial bench protocol in spiked aqueous samples, and notably higher recoveries from small volumes of human plasma. Clinical utility was demonstrated using patient samples infected with cytomegalovirus, monkeypox virus, and chikungunya virus, where quantitative PCR amplification results closely matched those from standard protocols. The authors conclude that this instrument-free extraction tool could serve as a practical bridge between existing nucleic acid amplification technologies and decentralized diagnostics in resource-limited settings.

Molecular Diagnostics of Infectious Diseases.

Takata M, Chikumi H, Yoshifuji A. Molecular Diagnostics of Infectious Diseases.. Yonago acta medica. 2026

This review examines the current landscape of molecular diagnostic technologies used to detect and characterize pathogens responsible for infectious diseases. The paper covers two primary approaches: nucleic acid amplification methods, including polymerase chain reaction variants and isothermal amplification techniques, and pathogen characterization through next-generation sequencing. Advances in polymerase chain reaction focus on improving speed, quantification, automation, and point-of-care applicability, while isothermal amplification methods offer simplified temperature requirements that make them particularly suited to decentralized testing settings. CRISPR-based detection systems are highlighted as an emerging complement to amplification methods, and next-generation sequencing is described as enabling the identification of novel pathogens, outbreak tracking, transmission monitoring, and microbiome analysis relevant to disease susceptibility. The authors conclude that future progress in infectious disease molecular diagnostics will come from integrating multiple technologies into hybrid platforms that address the current limitations of individual approaches.

QMini: A Portable Device for Infectious Disease Testing.

Mahbub N, Osborne M, Chen H et al.. QMini: A Portable Device for Infectious Disease Testing.. ACS nanoscience Au. 2026

The paper investigated the development of a portable device for nucleic acid-based infectious disease testing, aiming to overcome the size limitations of conventional diagnostic instruments that restrict their use in field settings. The researchers created the QMini by simplifying its fluidic module through direct pump-to-reaction-chamber connections and protecting its optical module from ambient light using a three-dimensional-printed enclosure. They applied the device to detect synthetic nucleic acid sequences from respiratory syncytial virus B, SARS-CoV-2, and influenza A using a bead-based assay with quantum dot barcodes. The QMini successfully detected low copy numbers of each target pathogen while showing minimal cross-reactivity against several nontarget viral nucleic acid sequences. The authors conclude that this miniaturized platform advances the portability of quantum dot barcode-based nucleic acid testing for potential use in decentralized infectious disease diagnostics.

Gut microbiota dynamics in free-range layer hens during outbreaks of spotty liver disease and fowl cholera—a field study

Friedrich Petranyi, Maria M. Whitton, Edina Lobo et al.. Gut microbiota dynamics in free-range layer hens during outbreaks of spotty liver disease and fowl cholera—a field study. Applied Microbiology and Biotechnology. 2026

This field study examined how gut microbiota dynamics, hen performance, and disease progression unfolded in a commercial free-range layer flock experiencing concurrent outbreaks of spotty liver disease and fowl cholera. Because such complex, multi-pathogen outbreak scenarios cannot be ethically or practically reproduced under controlled laboratory conditions, the researchers conducted observations directly on farm, tracking weekly performance metrics and microbial community composition across major gut sections. One group of hens received a precision biotic consisting of a synthetic glycan product, while a control group did not, allowing descriptive comparison of outcomes between the two. Supplemented birds showed associations with both higher egg output and higher cumulative mortality, alongside differences in intestinal microbial community composition and liver congestion compared to controls, though the unreplicated design limits causal interpretation. The study offers rare real-world observations of concurrent disease outbreaks in a production setting, providing ecological and microbial data intended to inform the design of future controlled investigations into disease dynamics and biotic interventions in poultry.

This review paper examines how the integration of multiomics technologies and artificial intelligence is reshaping precision drug discovery and clinical translation. The authors synthesize information across multiple omics layers — including genomics, transcriptomics, proteomics, and metabolomics — and assess how deep learning architectures can extract meaningful biological patterns from these complex, high-dimensional datasets. The review covers the application of this combined approach across the drug development pipeline, encompassing target identification, drug repositioning, de novo compound design, virtual screening, pharmacokinetic modeling, and safety assessment. Disease areas including oncology, neurodegeneration, and cardiovascular conditions are used to illustrate how these technologies converge to enable more precise therapeutic interventions. The authors also acknowledge persistent challenges such as harmonizing disparate data streams, ensuring reproducibility, and addressing algorithmic biases, while arguing that the fusion of multiomics and artificial intelligence marks a transformative new era in bench-to-clinic translational medicine.

Regulatory Challenges and Opportunities for Cell‐Derived Extracellular Vesicles in Pharmaceutical Development: A European and Global Perspective

Tania Limongi, Paola Milla, Barbara Stella et al.. Regulatory Challenges and Opportunities for Cell‐Derived Extracellular Vesicles in Pharmaceutical Development: A European and Global Perspective. Journal of Extracellular Vesicles. 2026

This paper examines the regulatory landscape governing cell-derived extracellular vesicles as therapeutic and diagnostic products, with a focus on how their acellular nature creates classification ambiguities across different jurisdictions. The authors analyze how major regulatory frameworks, including those of the European Union, the United States, and other regions, apply existing medicinal product categories to extracellular vesicle-based products in inconsistent ways, generating challenges around quality assessment, potency strategies, comparability, and cross-regional clinical development. A key focus is the European guidance clarifying that minimally modified extracellular vesicles fall outside the advanced therapy medicinal products definition, contrasting with the approach of treating such products as drugs and biologics subject to premarket review elsewhere. The authors argue that extracellular vesicle products can and should be developed within existing medicinal product frameworks, provided regulatory tools are applied consistently and outstanding grey zones in classification criteria and quality attributes are resolved through ongoing dialogue. Rather than proposing new normative frameworks, the review synthesizes the current state of regulatory and technical considerations to support harmonization efforts among international agencies and scientific societies.

Pairing AIS with RF: Analysis of a Markovian Approach for Maritime Data Fusion

Daniel Grimm, Kyra Jongman, Renato Cordeiro Ferreira et al.. Pairing AIS with RF: Analysis of a Markovian Approach for Maritime Data Fusion. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the challenge of monitoring maritime vessels by fusing two complementary data sources: the Automatic Identification System, which provides vessel identification but has known vulnerabilities, and space-based radio frequency observations, which detect vessel activity independently but cannot identify ships. Following a structured data science methodology, the researchers applied a Pair Hidden Markov Model to align individual radio frequency detections with AIS vessel trajectories by modeling temporal and spatial relationships, including noise and timing discrepancies. The approach was evaluated on real AIS data combined with synthetic radio frequency detections, benchmarked against nearest-neighbor baseline methods, and analyzed for systematic biases related to trajectory length, sampling density, stationarity, and spatial proximity. A log-odds variant of the model, which compares the full matching model against a null model lacking match transitions, substantially improved both precision and the ability to detect dark vessels, reducing several directional biases. However, a time-weighted nearest-neighbor baseline without any model fitting still outperformed the Pair Hidden Markov Model formulation, indicating that this single-detection approach approaches but does not surpass a well-designed heuristic.

Reconstruction of Dynamical Dark Energy and Gravity from latest cosmological data

Maria Luisa Gallavotti, Alessandra Silvestri. Reconstruction of Dynamical Dark Energy and Gravity from latest cosmological data. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether recent hints of non-standard cosmological expansion are accompanied by modifications to gravitational dynamics beyond the standard model. Using a joint non-parametric reconstruction approach, the authors simultaneously inferred the effective dark energy density and two gravitational response functions from a combination of CMB, baryon acoustic oscillation, supernova, and large-scale structure datasets. The analysis reveals a persistent feature in the dark energy density around redshift half to one, consistent with a crossing of the phantom divide, which proves robust across different data combinations and when gravitational modifications are included. The reconstructed gravitational response functions show suggestive but less statistically significant enhancements over a similar redshift range, though incorporating large-scale structure data shifts the lensing response toward values below unity while leaving the background feature largely intact. The authors conclude that current deviations from the standard cosmological model are most clearly manifested in the expansion history, while remaining open to correlated gravitational modifications that forthcoming Stage IV surveys could decisively test.

Understanding Contextual Bias in Agricultural CNNs Through Hidden-Neuron Concept Induction

Abhilekha Dalal, Avishek Das, Samatha Ereshi Akkamahadevi. Understanding Contextual Bias in Agricultural CNNs Through Hidden-Neuron Concept Induction. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigated how convolutional neural networks exploit contextual background information when classifying agricultural images, and whether the internal representations of hidden neurons can be made interpretable using a technique called Concept Induction. The researchers applied this approach across five agricultural datasets with varying degrees of contextual bias, examining four CNN architectures at multiple network depths and patch locations by encoding quantitative image descriptors into a knowledge graph and deriving logical expressions characterizing neuron activations. A substantial portion of neuron-feature pairs were statistically validated, with validation rates being highest at intermediate network depths and most strongly associated with edge, Gabor, and texture features. Notably, stronger contextual bias in a dataset did not correspond to higher neuron-feature validation rates, suggesting that a network's behavioral reliance on background context and the semantic interpretability of its hidden neurons are distinct phenomena. The authors conclude that Concept Induction offers a complementary lens to patch-based bias detection for understanding what CNN hidden layers actually represent.

KELP v1.0: an agent-ready Lagrangian particle tracker for unstructured meshes and HEALPix grids

Ivan Kuznetsov, Alexey Androsov, Dmitrii Pantiukhin et al.. KELP v1.0: an agent-ready Lagrangian particle tracker for unstructured meshes and HEALPix grids. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper presents KELP, a Lagrangian particle tracking software designed to follow virtual particles through ocean model velocity fields in order to study transport pathways, connectivity, and dispersion. The tool is specifically built to operate on unstructured ocean-model meshes and HEALPix equal-area grids, including those used by kilometre-scale digital twin ocean models, and is architecturally designed to be operated by coding agents powered by language models. The authors demonstrate that structured documentation and short task instructions called skills substantially improved the ability of language models to correctly prepare and execute Lagrangian studies, with completeness scores rising markedly across multiple tested models. KELP reproduced particle distributions consistent with other established trackers, and trajectories computed on HEALPix grids converged toward those on the native model mesh as grid resolution increased, supporting the use of HEALPix output for ensemble Lagrangian statistics when original mesh data are unavailable. The software demonstrated strong computational performance, handling very large particle ensembles and multi-decadal simulations, with an application to the Beaufort Gyre revealing that transport to Fram Strait occurs over timescales of years to decades.

Box-conditional Hypothesis Testing for Out-of-Distribution Object Detection

Donghoon Lee, Jusung Park, Shinjin Kang. Box-conditional Hypothesis Testing for Out-of-Distribution Object Detection. arXiv. 2026 preprint

The paper addresses the problem of detecting out-of-distribution objects in real-world object detection systems, framing the decision on each detected bounding box as a hypothesis test where the null hypothesis assumes the box contains an in-distribution object. The key insight is that the null distribution varies with box size and border contact, so a box should only be compared against in-distribution boxes sharing those same geometric properties, which provides a distribution-free bound on the false-alarm rate and reveals structure hidden by a single pooled null distribution. The authors also argue that features extracted before class-specific layers better preserve information about unknown objects, since later layers are trained exclusively on in-distribution classes and discard signals that could distinguish novel objects. Based on these principles, they propose PLUM, a training-free scoring method that pools backbone features from the last stage within a box and ranks their distance to in-distribution classes using box-conditional comparisons. PLUM achieves leading detection performance on standard benchmarks without requiring external models, outperforming compared methods on both PASCAL-VOC and BDD100K datasets.

Parity conservation of the isotropic galaxy 4PCF in redshift space

Chanasorn Kongprachaya, Nicola Bartolo, Daniele Bertacca et al.. Parity conservation of the isotropic galaxy 4PCF in redshift space. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether Redshift Space Distortions (RSD) could mimic a parity-violating signal in measurements of the galaxy four-point correlation function (4PCF), which has recently been used to search for cosmological parity violation. The authors analyze the perturbative relativistic galaxy number density to third order in Standard Eulerian Perturbation Theory, incorporating biased tracer expansion, tidal bias, wide-angle effects through a local line-of-sight definition, and generalization to arbitrary cosmological backgrounds. By expanding the 4PCF in spherical harmonics, they demonstrate that angular momentum selection rules encoded in Wigner 3-j symbols force all parity-odd contributions from RSD to vanish when averaged over all line-of-sight orientations, in both the plane-parallel and full wide-angle cases. This result holds up to leading order in general relativistic corrections and applies to both the configuration-space 4PCF and its Fourier-space counterpart, the trispectrum. The finding is significant because it supports the interpretation that any observed parity-violation signal in isotropic 4PCF estimators cannot be attributed to a systematic artefact from RSD, lending greater credibility to such detections as potentially physical in origin.

Diagnostic performance of antigen-based rapid test kits for six respiratory viruses in children: comparison with a multiplex PCR panel and correlation with viral load.

Kitagawa D, Di Lorenzo A, Okamura T et al.. Diagnostic performance of antigen-based rapid test kits for six respiratory viruses in children: comparison with a multiplex PCR panel and correlation with viral load.. Journal of clinical microbiology. 2026

This study evaluated the diagnostic accuracy of antigen-based rapid test kits for six common pediatric respiratory viruses—SARS-CoV-2, influenza A, influenza B, adenovirus, human metapneumovirus, and respiratory syncytial virus—comparing them against a multiplex PCR reference panel. In a prospective paired-specimen design, nasopharyngeal swabs from children presenting with respiratory tract infections were tested in parallel by both methods, with positive specimens subjected to quantitative real-time PCR to determine viral load. Sensitivity varied considerably across viral targets, ranging from low for adenovirus to moderate for human metapneumovirus, while specificity exceeded ninety-nine percent for all six viruses. Critically, antigen test sensitivity was strongly dependent on viral load, approaching ninety to one hundred percent at high viral concentrations but falling below fifty percent at low viral loads, a pattern that was consistent across all six pathogens. The authors conclude that rapid antigen tests reliably identify children carrying the highest viral burdens—those most likely to have acute symptomatic infection—and that viral load is the principal determinant of antigen test performance regardless of the specific respiratory virus being targeted.

Diagnostic yield of reflex multiplex PCR after a negative rapid antigen test in children hospitalized with presumed viral acute respiratory tract infection: a prospective cohort study.

Bedir AZ, Saf C, Yörük MA. Diagnostic yield of reflex multiplex PCR after a negative rapid antigen test in children hospitalized with presumed viral acute respiratory tract infection: a prospective cohort study.. Irish journal of medical science. 2026

The study examined whether adding reflex multiplex PCR testing after a negative rapid antigen test improves viral detection in children hospitalized with presumed viral acute respiratory infections. In a single-center prospective cohort, children first underwent a five-target rapid antigen test, and those testing negative could then receive multiplex PCR at physician discretion. Rapid antigen testing alone identified only a minority of viral cases, while reflex PCR among antigen-negative children who were tested returned a high proportion of positive results, with rhinovirus/enterovirus—not covered by the antigen panel—being the most commonly identified pathogen. A negative antigen test therefore could not exclude viral infection, and reflex PCR substantially increased diagnostic yield, though selective use of the follow-on test means the reported yield is a lower bound. Antibiotic use was high regardless of whether a virus was detected, and the appropriateness of this prescribing could not be assessed because systematic bacterial testing was not performed.

Diagnostic Accuracy of the RapidFor™ RSV/ADV Rapid Test Kit Compared with PCR for the Detection of Respiratory Syncytial Virus and Adenovirus in Nasopharyngeal Swab Samples

Ates B, Kacar A, Kok S et al.. Diagnostic Accuracy of the RapidFor™ RSV/ADV Rapid Test Kit Compared with PCR for the Detection of Respiratory Syncytial Virus and Adenovirus in Nasopharyngeal Swab Samples. 2026

This prospective study evaluated the diagnostic accuracy of the RapidFor™ RSV/ADV Rapid Test Kit for detecting respiratory syncytial virus and adenovirus in children presenting to a pediatric emergency department with acute respiratory symptoms, using multiplex real-time PCR as the reference standard. Nasopharyngeal swabs were collected from children aged eighteen years and under, with the rapid antigen test performed at the point of care alongside PCR testing. The rapid test demonstrated very high sensitivity and specificity for both pathogens, with excellent agreement with PCR results for adenovirus and RSV alike. Age-related patterns emerged, with RSV positivity concentrated among infants under one year and adenovirus positivity peaking in school-aged children, while conjunctival injection and fever independently predicted adenovirus infection and cough with young age predicted RSV. The authors conclude that the kit's ability to simultaneously detect both pathogens from a single sample with high accuracy supports its potential utility for timely clinical decision-making in pediatric emergency settings.

Can exercise affect immune function to increase susceptibility to infection?

Richard J. Simpson, Richard J. Simpson, Maree Gleeson et al.. Can exercise affect immune function to increase susceptibility to infection?. PubMed. 2026

This debate article examined the longstanding question of whether exercise itself can suppress immune function and increase susceptibility to infection. The paper pitted opposing arguments against each other on several contested issues, including whether high-performance athletes are genuinely more infection-prone than the general population, whether exercise independently alters immunity beyond confounding factors such as sleep disruption, nutritional deficits, and psychological stress, and whether commonly used biomarkers adequately reflect immune health in individuals performing arduous exercise. Moderate-intensity exercise of relatively short duration is broadly accepted as beneficial for immune defense, particularly in older and chronically ill individuals, whereas the immune consequences of the extreme training loads undertaken by elite athletes and military personnel remain disputed. Both sides agreed that infection susceptibility is multifactorial in origin, but whether exercise per se is a direct causative factor of elevated infection risk in high-performance populations remains unresolved, and the authors call for more rigorous empirical research to clarify this central question in exercise immunology.

The Dual Role of Interleukin-6 in the Pathophysiology of Skeletal Muscle: Mechanisms, Challenges, and Therapeutic Prospects

Yingyu Wang, J Zhang, Jie Wang et al.. The Dual Role of Interleukin-6 in the Pathophysiology of Skeletal Muscle: Mechanisms, Challenges, and Therapeutic Prospects. Pharmaceuticals. 2026

This review examines the paradoxical roles of interleukin-6 in skeletal muscle, where the same cytokine serves both protective and destructive functions depending on context. Under physiological conditions, particularly during acute exercise, contracting muscle releases IL-6 as a myokine that regulates energy metabolism, insulin sensitivity, and muscle regeneration through autocrine, paracrine, and endocrine signaling. In contrast, chronically elevated IL-6 levels arising from pathological states such as cancer cachexia, sepsis, muscular dystrophy, and disuse atrophy drive muscle wasting, metabolic dysfunction, and functional decline. The review systematically dissects the downstream signaling cascades involved, including the JAK/STAT, MAPK, and PI3K-Akt pathways, and distinguishes between classical IL-6 signaling and trans-signaling as mechanistically distinct modes of action. The authors also identify key research challenges and propose therapeutic strategies targeting the IL-6 pathway, including monoclonal antibodies, kinase inhibitors, and lifestyle-based interventions, as a basis for future translational and clinical efforts.

Spinal cord injury: Known and possible influences on the immune response to exercise

Christof A. Leicht, Victoria Louise Goosey-Tolfrey, NICOLETTE CLAIRE BISHOP. Spinal cord injury: Known and possible influences on the immune response to exercise. PubMed. 2026

This review paper examined how spinal cord injury affects immune function, with particular focus on how exercise influences immune responses in this population. The authors conducted a comprehensive literature search to gather existing research on exercise immunology in individuals with spinal cord injury, spanning patient, recreationally active, and athlete groups. A central concern of the review is that autonomic dysfunction following spinal cord injury alters resting and post-exercise catecholamine concentrations, which may directly suppress immunity given that the majority of lymphoid cells express adrenergic receptors, with this effect being especially pronounced in individuals with tetraplegia. However, the authors note that some immune responses to exercise, such as increases in natural killer cell cytotoxicity and mucosal immunity changes in tetraplegic individuals, do not follow the expected catecholamine-dependent pattern, suggesting compensatory mechanisms involving sympathetic reflex activity, receptor hypersensitivity, or parasympathetic and neuroendocrine adjustments may offset disability-related immune deficits. The authors conclude that exercise and rehabilitation can enhance certain measures of immunity and autonomic function following spinal cord injury, and call for further research to clarify the underlying mechanisms and their implications for infection susceptibility.

Replaying germinal center evolution on a quantified affinity landscape

William S. DeWitt, Ashni A. Vora, Tatsuya Araki et al.. Replaying germinal center evolution on a quantified affinity landscape. Cell. 2026

The paper investigated the evolutionary dynamics of affinity maturation in germinal centers, where B cells undergo Darwinian selection to produce higher-affinity antibodies. The researchers developed an experimental evolution model that replayed over one hundred monoclonal germinal center reactions and used deep mutational scanning to assign affinity values to individual cells, thereby mapping the affinity landscape. They found that germinal centers achieve predictable evolutionary outcomes despite noisy selection, with the exploration of the affinity landscape being heavily constrained by somatic hypermutation biases. By inferring a fitness landscape that quantitatively recapitulates the affinity maturation trajectory, the study also revealed that commonly observed features such as tolerance for low-affinity lineages and rapid affinity plateauing are likely artifacts of survivorship bias rather than true properties of germinal center selection.

Accelerating protein design by scaling experimental characterization

Jason Qian, Lukas F. Milles, Basile I. M. Wicky et al.. Accelerating protein design by scaling experimental characterization. Nature Communications. 2026

This paper addressed the bottleneck of experimental validation in de novo protein design, where computational methods have outpaced the capacity of standard biochemical workflows to test candidate proteins. The authors developed Semi-Automated Protein Production (SAPP), a modular and scalable protocol that enables production and standardized characterization of hundreds of protein designs per day using common laboratory equipment, with full end-to-end execution completed within two days. They also created a barcoding and demultiplexing protocol to reduce gene synthesis costs by leveraging oligonucleotide pools to generate thousands of sequence-verified clones. The platform was validated by screening redesigned fluorescent proteins and by identifying de novo protein binders capable of potently neutralizing respiratory syncytial virus. Together, these workflows increase experimental throughput by at least an order of magnitude while reducing hands-on bench time, and are designed to be broadly adoptable with or without open-source robotics.

Understanding graft-versus-host disease. Preliminary findings regarding the effects of exercise in affected patients

Carmen Fiuza‐Luces, Nuria Garatachea, Nathan A. Berger et al.. Understanding graft-versus-host disease. Preliminary findings regarding the effects of exercise in affected patients. PubMed. 2026

This paper examines graft-versus-host disease, a potentially multi-systemic complication arising when donor lymphocytes attack host tissues following allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation, with a particular focus on its chronic form and its impact on patients' physical capacity. The authors reviewed the pathophysiological mechanisms underlying the disease alongside available evidence from both animal models and human studies on the effects of exercise in affected patients. Their review suggests that exercise training as an adjunct therapy holds promise for improving physical fitness and immune function while reducing fatigue in transplant recipients. However, the authors conclude that the evidence base remains insufficient, calling for more randomized controlled trials specifically targeting patients with graft-versus-host disease.

Pairing AIS with RF: Analysis of a Markovian Approach for Maritime Data Fusion

Daniel Grimm, Kyra Jongman, Renato Cordeiro Ferreira et al.. Pairing AIS with RF: Analysis of a Markovian Approach for Maritime Data Fusion. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the challenge of monitoring maritime activity by fusing two complementary data sources: the Automatic Identification System, which provides vessel identification but has known vulnerabilities, and space-based radio frequency observations, which offer independent detection of vessel activity but lack identification capability. Following a structured data science methodology, the researchers developed a Pair Hidden Markov Model to align individual radio frequency detections with AIS trajectories by jointly modeling temporal and spatial information along with noise and timing discrepancies. The approach was evaluated on real AIS data combined with synthetic radio frequency detections, with performance assessed against nearest-neighbor baselines and analyzed for systematic biases related to trajectory length, sampling density, stationarity, and spatial separation. A log-odds scoring variant that compares the full model against a null model without match transitions substantially improved precision and the ability to detect vessels behaving covertly, reducing several of the biases present in the base model. Despite these gains, a time-weighted nearest-neighbor baseline without any model fitting remained superior across both metrics, indicating that this single-detection Pair Hidden Markov Model formulation closely approaches but does not surpass a well-designed heuristic method.

Reconstruction of Dynamical Dark Energy and Gravity from latest cosmological data

Maria Luisa Gallavotti, Alessandra Silvestri. Reconstruction of Dynamical Dark Energy and Gravity from latest cosmological data. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether recent hints of non-standard cosmological expansion are accompanied by modifications to gravitational dynamics, going beyond simple dark energy parameterizations. The authors perform a joint, non-parametric reconstruction of the effective dark energy density and two gravitational response functions governing matter clustering and lensing, using a combination of CMB, baryon acoustic oscillation, supernova, and large-scale structure datasets. They find a persistent feature in the dark energy density around redshift 0.5–0.7 that corresponds to a crossing of the phantom divide, which appears robustly across different data combinations and persists when gravitational modifications are allowed. The gravitational response functions show suggestive enhancements over a similar redshift range, though these are less statistically significant, and including large-scale structure data instead favors a suppressed lensing response. The authors conclude that current deviations from the standard cosmological model are most clearly manifest in the expansion history, while correlated modifications to gravity remain plausible but await confirmation from future surveys.

Understanding Contextual Bias in Agricultural CNNs Through Hidden-Neuron Concept Induction

Abhilekha Dalal, Avishek Das, Samatha Ereshi Akkamahadevi. Understanding Contextual Bias in Agricultural CNNs Through Hidden-Neuron Concept Induction. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates how convolutional neural networks exploit contextual background information when classifying agricultural images, and whether the internal representations of hidden neurons can be made interpretable through a technique called Concept Induction. The researchers applied this approach across five agricultural datasets with varying degrees of contextual bias, analyzing four CNN architectures at multiple network depths and patch locations by encoding quantitative image descriptors into a knowledge graph and deriving logical expressions that characterize neuron activations. Validation of these expressions using statistical tests revealed that a substantial minority of neuron-feature pairs were confirmed, with the highest validation rates occurring at intermediate network depths and for texture-related features such as edge, Gabor, and Tamura descriptors. Notably, the degree of contextual bias in a dataset did not align with the rate of neuron-feature validation, suggesting that a network's behavioral reliance on background context and the semantic interpretability of its hidden neurons are distinct phenomena. The authors conclude that Concept Induction offers a complementary lens to patch-based bias detection for understanding what CNNs actually learn.

KELP v1.0: an agent-ready Lagrangian particle tracker for unstructured meshes and HEALPix grids

Ivan Kuznetsov, Alexey Androsov, Dmitrii Pantiukhin et al.. KELP v1.0: an agent-ready Lagrangian particle tracker for unstructured meshes and HEALPix grids. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper introduces KELP v1.0, a Lagrangian particle tracking software designed to follow virtual particles through ocean model velocity fields in order to study transport pathways, connectivity, and dispersion. KELP operates on unstructured ocean-model meshes of triangular, quadrilateral, and mixed types, as well as on HEALPix equal-area grids used by kilometre-scale ocean digital twins, computing both two- and three-dimensional trajectories. A central design goal is compatibility with coding agents, whereby language models autonomously prepare, configure, and run experiments using structured task instructions called skills, and an evaluation showed these skills substantially improved the completeness of study files generated by multiple language models. The software was validated by reproducing particle distributions from two independent trackers, and trajectories computed on HEALPix grids were shown to converge toward those on the original model mesh as pixel resolution was refined, supporting the use of HEALPix output for Lagrangian ensemble studies when native mesh data are unavailable. A multi-decade simulation tracking millions of particles in the Beaufort Gyre demonstrated both the computational efficiency and scientific applicability of the tool.

Box-conditional Hypothesis Testing for Out-of-Distribution Object Detection

Donghoon Lee, Jusung Park, Shinjin Kang. Box-conditional Hypothesis Testing for Out-of-Distribution Object Detection. arXiv. 2026 preprint

The paper addresses the problem of detecting out-of-distribution objects in real-world object detection systems, framing each detection decision as a hypothesis test where the null hypothesis assumes a detected box contains an in-distribution object. The authors identify that the null distribution of box features varies with box size and border contact with the image edge, motivating a conditional testing approach that compares detected boxes only against in-distribution boxes sharing the same geometric properties. This conditioning provides a distribution-free bound on false-alarm rates and reveals discriminative structure obscured when all boxes are pooled together. Rather than using features from classification layers that discard information about unknown objects, the method extracts features from the final backbone stage before class-specific processing. The resulting training-free scoring method, called PLUM, achieves leading out-of-distribution detection performance on standard benchmarks without requiring external models, outperforming compared methods on both PASCAL-VOC and BDD100K datasets.

On numerical issues of divergences between nearly equal distributions

Brandon Amos. On numerical issues of divergences between nearly equal distributions. arXiv. 2026 preprint

The paper investigates numerical instability problems that arise when computing divergences between categorical distributions that are nearly equal. Building on prior work identifying similar issues in softmax and soft cross-entropy loss, the authors demonstrate that forward and reverse Kullback-Leibler divergence, generalized Jensen-Shannon divergence, Monte Carlo KL estimates, and entropy all suffer from rounding and cancellation errors under these conditions. These issues affect both forward computations and gradient computations, with practical consequences for machine learning applications such as knowledge distillation, reinforcement learning, and proximal optimization methods. The authors propose numerically stable reformulations for each of these divergence operations and provide validated example implementations tested through property-based testing.

Advances in Diagnostic Technologies of Major Avian Respiratory Viruses: A Review.

Hu Z, Hailai A, He H et al.. Advances in Diagnostic Technologies of Major Avian Respiratory Viruses: A Review.. Veterinary sciences. 2026

This review examines diagnostic technologies for four major avian respiratory viruses — avian influenza virus, Newcastle disease virus, infectious bronchitis virus, and infectious laryngotracheitis virus — which pose serious threats to the poultry industry and, in some cases, to public health through cross-species transmission. The authors synthesize and compare a broad range of molecular and immunological detection methods, evaluating them across performance metrics such as sensitivity, multiplexing capacity, turnaround time, and suitability for point-of-care use. Techniques covered include real-time quantitative PCR and its multiplex formats, isothermal amplification approaches, CRISPR-Cas-based detection systems, high-throughput sequencing, microarray technologies, and immunological assays enhanced by nanomaterials and engineered antibodies. Integrated and portable platforms such as microfluidics and smartphone-assisted systems are also discussed. The review concludes by identifying future directions in avian diagnostics, aiming to provide a practical reference for developing more efficient and accessible disease surveillance and early warning systems.

Advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarkers, 2020-26: scientific evidence, recommendations, gaps, and developmental priorities.

Walzl G, McHugh TD, Chakaya J et al.. Advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarkers, 2020-26: scientific evidence, recommendations, gaps, and developmental priorities.. The Lancet. Microbe. 2027

This paper reviews major advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarker research over roughly a five-year period, synthesizing evidence that has shaped updated WHO policy guidance consolidating recommendations for disease detection and drug-resistance testing into a single framework. The work covers progress across the full diagnostic cascade, including digital chest radiography with computer-aided detection as a frontline screening tool, expanded decentralized molecular testing through automated and manual nucleic acid amplification platforms, and novel specimen types such as tongue swabs and stool that improve bacteriological confirmation in children and sputum-scarce populations. Drug-resistance testing has been strengthened by targeted molecular assays and the recognition of next-generation sequencing as a WHO-endorsed reference laboratory approach, while urine-based lipoarabinomannan assays continue to offer incremental benefit in people with HIV and advanced disease despite performance limitations. Host blood transcriptomic and other biomarker signatures demonstrate promise for prognosis and triage, yet no biomarker has met WHO target product profile criteria for a point-of-care test capable of reliably distinguishing infection from disease, including subclinical tuberculosis. The authors conclude that substantial gaps in test performance, implementation, and equitable access remain, and identify these as priority areas for further development.

MicroRNA-based point-of-care diagnostics for emerging and re-emerging infectious diseases: from biomarker discovery to clinical implementation.

Cappleman V, Gupta H. MicroRNA-based point-of-care diagnostics for emerging and re-emerging infectious diseases: from biomarker discovery to clinical implementation.. Expert review of molecular diagnostics. 2026

This review examined the potential of microRNAs as host-response biomarkers for diagnosing emerging and re-emerging infectious diseases, with a focus on translating research findings into deployable clinical tools. The authors evaluated miRNA diagnostic potential across a range of viral diseases including malaria, dengue, chikungunya, Zika, Crimean-Congo hemorrhagic fever, Japanese encephalitis, and Nipah virus disease. Rather than focusing solely on biomarker discovery or detection technology, the review took a cross-disease translational perspective connecting miRNA signatures to analytical validation, point-of-care platform development, economic considerations, and deployment in resource-limited settings. The authors found that progress from differential-expression studies to clinically usable diagnostics remains constrained, and argue that the field should shift toward validating reproducible multi-miRNA signatures through standardized workflows and prospective multicentre trials. They conclude that clinical adoption will ultimately require integrating these signatures into affordable, sample-to-answer platforms and demonstrating their utility in improving real-world clinical decisions.

Sample-to-answer automated centrifugal microfluidics with computer-vision readout for point-of-care molecular diagnostics

Akhtar AS, Lapins N, Moura JM et al.. Sample-to-answer automated centrifugal microfluidics with computer-vision readout for point-of-care molecular diagnostics. 2026

The paper addresses the critical need for decentralized diagnostic tools exposed by the COVID-19 pandemic, particularly in settings where centralized laboratory infrastructure is limited. The researchers developed a fully automated centrifugal microfluidic platform that integrates loop-mediated isothermal amplification with computer-vision-based optical readout, requiring only a single button press to execute the complete sample-to-answer workflow. The system was demonstrated for SARS-CoV-2 detection using RNA fragments spiked into water and artificial saliva, achieving consistent detection at low copy numbers per reaction while processing dozens of samples within under an hour. The platform consolidates all assay steps—heating, fluid manipulation via rotation, and signal detection—without user intervention, making it suitable for point-of-care deployment. The authors propose that this architecture is broadly generalizable to other infectious disease targets, offering a scalable template for rapid molecular diagnostics during future outbreaks.

Syphilis/HIV Early Rapid Response (SHIVER): Community-Driven Point-of-Care Testing (POCT) Across Diverse Sites in Saskatchewan, Canada.

Spence C, Rampersad V, Williamson N et al.. Syphilis/HIV Early Rapid Response (SHIVER): Community-Driven Point-of-Care Testing (POCT) Across Diverse Sites in Saskatchewan, Canada.. Diagnostics (Basel, Switzerland). 2026

The SHIVER project studied access to syphilis and HIV testing among underserved, rural, remote, and inner-city populations in Saskatchewan, Canada, where both infections have been increasing disproportionately. The program implemented a community-driven point-of-care testing and treatment model in which participants aged sixteen and older were offered dual rapid testing in a single encounter, with immediate treatment offered to those testing positive for syphilis and expedited linkage to care for those with reactive HIV results. Enrollment over the study period included a substantial proportion of first-time testers for both infections, and the program achieved notable diagnostic yields for syphilis and HIV. The integration of both tests into one encounter reduced procedural burden and improved case detection, while community partnerships and culturally safe service delivery were central to the model's implementation. The authors conclude that this flexible approach is scalable and can serve as a model for expanding infectious disease care in populations facing the greatest barriers to testing and treatment.

Sequential diagnosis with language models

Harsha Nori, Mayank Daswani, Christopher J. Kelly et al.. Sequential diagnosis with language models. Science. 2026

The paper investigated whether frontier language models could perform sequential diagnosis, a process that mirrors how clinicians iteratively gather information and refine their thinking rather than diagnosing from a single snapshot. The researchers built an interactive benchmark and an AI system designed to simulate a panel of physicians, which progressively updated a differential diagnosis and selected cost-effective tests given an initial brief clinical presentation. The system was evaluated on two datasets of clinical cases, one drawn from a prestigious medical journal and one from real emergency department admissions. The AI system substantially outperformed generalist physicians on diagnostic accuracy across both datasets while operating within a framework that also tracked workup cost. The authors conclude that these results, though obtained under controlled benchmark conditions, suggest meaningful potential for language models to support more accurate and cost-efficient clinical diagnosis.

Towards autonomous medical artificial intelligence agents

Dyke Ferber, Lars Hilgers, Christiane Höper et al.. Towards autonomous medical artificial intelligence agents. Nature. 2026

The paper studied whether an autonomous artificial intelligence agent could manage complete patient cases within an electronic health record environment at a level comparable to physicians. The researchers developed MIRA, an agent operating in a sandboxed electronic health record system with access to a broad clinical action space, enabling it to gather patient histories, order and interpret diagnostic tests, generate differential diagnoses, and formulate treatment plans including prescriptions and admission planning. Testing the system on simulations based on real patient cases spanning multiple diagnoses, MIRA outperformed physicians in diagnostic accuracy and made decisions that were guideline-concordant, medication-safe, and appropriate for admission. Unlike prior large language model applications that addressed only isolated clinical subtasks or offered free-text recommendations, MIRA demonstrated the ability to translate clinical intent into structured, actionable operations within the electronic health record. The authors conclude that such an integrated agent could serve as a more effective decision-support partner for physicians, while acknowledging that prospective real-world studies are needed to establish its generalization, safety, and governance.

MedicalAgentsBench for complex medical reasoning: Comparing internalized reasoning models versus externalized agent-based frameworks

Yanjun Shao, Xiangru Tang, Jiwoong Sohn et al.. MedicalAgentsBench for complex medical reasoning: Comparing internalized reasoning models versus externalized agent-based frameworks. Patterns. 2026

This paper investigated whether internalized reasoning models and externalized agent-based frameworks represent competing or complementary approaches to complex medical question answering. The researchers introduced a curated benchmark of challenging clinical questions drawn from multiple medical datasets, applying difficulty-aware filtering and contamination screening to ensure meaningful evaluation. They tested a broad range of internalized reasoning models, standard base models, and agent-based scaffolding methods against this benchmark. The key finding is that both approaches independently improve performance, but their benefits are additive, with the best results achieved by combining an internalized reasoning model with an agent workflow. A cost-performance analysis further demonstrated that this combination dominates the efficiency frontier, while lightweight optimization strategies offer viable options for resource-constrained deployments.

Multi-omics and artificial intelligence for precision drug discovery and potential clinical applications

Yuqing Liu, Kun Zhu, Weijun Peng et al.. Multi-omics and artificial intelligence for precision drug discovery and potential clinical applications. Signal Transduction and Targeted Therapy. 2026

This review examines how the integration of multiomics technologies and artificial intelligence is reshaping precision drug discovery and translational medicine. The authors survey a broad range of multiomics approaches — including genomic, transcriptomic, proteomic, and metabolomic profiling — alongside deep learning methodologies to assess their combined utility across the drug development pipeline. The review covers applications such as target deconvolution, drug repositioning, de novo compound discovery, virtual screening, pharmacokinetic modeling, and safety assessment, with illustrative cases drawn from oncology, neurodegenerative diseases, and cardiovascular conditions. The authors argue that this convergence accelerates target identification and lead optimization while enabling more precisely designed clinical trials, though they acknowledge persistent challenges including harmonization of disparate data streams, reproducibility, and mitigation of algorithmic biases. Overall, the review advances the view that multiomics–AI integration represents a foundational shift toward data-driven, molecularly informed therapeutic development.

Parity conservation of the isotropic galaxy 4PCF in redshift space

Chanasorn Kongprachaya, Nicola Bartolo, Daniele Bertacca et al.. Parity conservation of the isotropic galaxy 4PCF in redshift space. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether Redshift Space Distortions (RSD), including leading-order general relativistic corrections, could mimic a parity-violating signal in measurements of the galaxy four-point correlation function (4PCF). The authors analyzed the perturbative relativistic galaxy number density using Standard Eulerian Perturbation Theory to third order, incorporating wide-angle effects, tidal bias, and an arbitrary cosmological background. By expanding the 4PCF in spherical harmonics, they demonstrated that angular momentum selection rules encoded in Wigner 3-j symbols force all parity-odd contributions from RSD to vanish when averaged over all line-of-sight orientations, in both the plane-parallel and full wide-angle cases. This result holds for the isotropic 4PCF estimator commonly used in observational surveys and extends prior trispectrum analyses in Fourier space. The finding is significant because it supports the interpretation that any detected parity-violation signal in such analyses cannot be attributed to a systematic RSD artefact.

Dynamic bigraphical Matérn--Whittle processes: learning dependence graphs among spatial-temporal fields

Alokesh Manna, Debangan Dey. Dynamic bigraphical Matérn--Whittle processes: learning dependence graphs among spatial-temporal fields. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the problem of learning time-varying dependence structures among multiple spatiotemporal variables recorded at shared locations, such as pollutant concentrations or disease case counts across monitoring networks. The authors develop dynamic bigraphical Matérn–Whittle processes, a framework in which each variable is represented by a spatial field evolving through autoregression, while a time-varying signed graph couples the temporal innovations across variables, allowing links to appear, disappear, and change sign over time. A key theoretical contribution is that the joint distribution of latent fields factorizes over eigenmodes of a graph Laplacian, making the normalizing constant independent of the graph and enabling exact computation of the time-varying graph posterior via forward–backward recursions. In simulations, the proposed method recovers dependence graphs with higher precision than competing approaches including graphical lasso variants and Poisson log-normal networks, and it also yields better out-of-sample predictions than standard spatiotemporal models fit variable by variable. Applied to air-quality data and German infectious disease surveillance, the method successfully recovers known seasonal sign changes between pollutants and identifies stable positive inter-disease links that persist through the pandemic period.

Constrained Stochastic Policy Targeting under Heterogeneous Partial Interference

Elena Dal Torrione, Chan Park, Laura Forastiere. Constrained Stochastic Policy Targeting under Heterogeneous Partial Interference. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the design of optimal treatment policies in settings where individuals exhibit heterogeneous interference — meaning that treating one person affects others in ways that depend on individual characteristics. The authors study two related constrained optimization problems: learning individualized stochastic treatment policies that minimize average treatment probability while meeting a specified outcome target, and the complementary problem of maximizing outcomes subject to a resource constraint. They develop an estimation framework using a smooth surrogate for constraint violations combined with interference-aware inverse-probability-weighting and self-normalized estimators, providing finite-sample regret guarantees for both approaches. Applying the methodology to a Colombian conditional cash transfer program targeting school attendance, they demonstrate that individualizing treatment probabilities and accounting for interference can meaningfully reduce resource use compared to policies that either ignore interference or apply homogeneous treatment rules.

Extending an X-ray & Mid-Infrared diagnostic for absorption in Active Galactic Nuclei to higher redshifts using NuSTAR and XMM-Newton

Ryan J. Turney, Claire L. Greenwell, David M. Alexander. Extending an X-ray & Mid-Infrared diagnostic for absorption in Active Galactic Nuclei to higher redshifts using NuSTAR and XMM-Newton. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigated how to measure the fraction of heavily obscured, Compton-thick active galactic nuclei across cosmic time, as this fraction constrains models of supermassive black hole growth and evolution. The authors extended an existing diagnostic tool — based on the ratio of observed X-ray to mid-infrared luminosity — from the local universe to higher redshifts by applying it to a sample of bright AGN drawn from a long-baseline NuSTAR catalogue with a median redshift around a third. X-ray spectral fitting using physically motivated models was performed on the NuSTAR sources, supplemented by XMM-Newton data, to constrain the hydrogen column densities needed to calibrate the diagnostic. The high-redshift relationship between the luminosity ratio and column density was found to be broadly consistent with the low-redshift version, with modest parameter differences attributable to the higher X-ray luminosities in the NuSTAR sample rather than genuine redshift evolution. Applying the calibrated model to the full catalogue yielded an observed Compton-thick fraction consistent with recent independent measurements from Swift/BAT at low redshift, supporting the robustness of this extended diagnostic approach.

MamaReason: Unfinished Reasoning and Answer Recovery in Clinical Language Models

Thanni Adewuyi, Adepoju Jeremiah Moyondafoluwa, Dorcas Amapu et al.. MamaReason: Unfinished Reasoning and Answer Recovery in Clinical Language Models. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates a specific failure mode in clinical language models where incomplete reasoning traces already contain the correct diagnosis before the model formally produces an answer. The researchers developed MamaReason, a model adapted for maternal and child health questions, and evaluated it on a benchmark of paired clinical presentations. Using an explicit-commitment extractor, they found that the vast majority of failed reasoning passes contained an identifiable diagnosis within the unfinished trace, and that answer continuation most often simply echoed that already-committed diagnosis. The study further showed that reading these embedded commitments directly, without additional generation, recovered most of the cases that full continuation would have restored, suggesting that diagnostic content and answer completion are separable phenomena. The authors argue that clinical language model evaluation must distinguish between diagnostic information present in a reasoning trace, the completion of a formally accepted answer, and overall paired diagnostic correctness, as these represent meaningfully different outcomes obscured by standard single-readout evaluation.

A Multi-Layered AI Pipeline for BPMN Generation from Natural Language

Alexandru Rancea, Ionut Anghel, Tudor Cioara et al.. A Multi-Layered AI Pipeline for BPMN Generation from Natural Language. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the challenge of automatically converting unstructured natural language process descriptions into formal Business Process Model and Notation diagrams, a task complicated by linguistic ambiguity and the difficulty of detecting non-adjacent control-flow relationships. The authors developed a multi-layered pipeline that separates the problem into dedicated components: a fine-tuned transformer-based model for entity extraction, a large language model-based control-flow engine for capturing non-adjacent relationships, and an ontology population mechanism to represent the resulting process models as semantic knowledge structures. To compensate for limited training data, the pipeline employs a dynamic few-shot strategy with controlled template input construction to improve generalization. The approach was evaluated on literature benchmarks, industry reference processes, and a real-world transitional care process for heart disease patients serving as an expert-defined benchmark. Results show strong performance in relation extraction and structural alignment between generated and reference BPMN models across multiple evaluation scenarios.

WHO target product profiles for dengue therapeutics: an interdisciplinary analysis of ethical, regulatory, and implementation considerations.

Johnson T, Loganathan S, Jamrozik E et al.. WHO target product profiles for dengue therapeutics: an interdisciplinary analysis of ethical, regulatory, and implementation considerations.. The Lancet. Microbe. 2027

This paper examined the urgent need for dengue therapeutics in the context of a historically unprecedented global surge in dengue cases, driven by climate and demographic shifts. The authors conducted an interdisciplinary analysis of ethical, regulatory, and implementation considerations to support the World Health Organization's development of target product profiles for dengue treatments. Special attention was given to populations at heightened risk of severe disease, including those with multimorbidity, immunosuppression, and socioeconomic disadvantage, for whom existing live-attenuated vaccines are unsuitable. The analysis identified critical needs including improved surveillance to characterize at-risk groups, co-produced research approaches that meaningfully include these populations, and strategies to overcome barriers to access such as the availability of rapid diagnostics. The authors propose that this framework could also inform the equitable development and delivery of countermeasures for other infectious disease threats beyond dengue.
